   <div class="marriage-line-margin2px">&#8203;</div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;margin-bottom:1px;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;1976&#59;&#32;<abbr title="divorced">div.</abbr>&#160;2008&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Domestic partner</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Julieanne_Newbould" title="Julieanne Newbould">Julieanne Newbould</a> (since 1998)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Children</th><td class="infobox-data">4</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Residence(s)</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/Potts_Point" class="mw-redirect" title="Potts Point">Potts Point</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a>, Australia</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Education</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/LaSalle_Catholic_College,_Bankstown" title="LaSalle Catholic College, Bankstown">De La Salle Catholic College</a></li><li>Belmore Technical College</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Profession</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li>Trade unionist</li><li>Politician</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Website</th><td class="infobox-data"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.paulkeating.net.au">www.paulkeating.net.au</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>

<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Government_of_Australia" title="Government of Australia"><img alt="Government of Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="31" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Paul_Keating_sidebar" title="Template:Paul Keating sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Paul_Keating_sidebar" title="Template talk:Paul Keating sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Paul_Keating_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Paul John Keating</b> (born 18 January 1944) is an Australian former politician who served as the 24th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1991 to 1996, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> (ALP). He previously served as the <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">treasurer of Australia</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Hawke_government" title="Hawke government">Hawke government</a> from 1983 to 1991 and as the 7th <a href="/wiki/Deputy_prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy prime minister of Australia">deputy prime minister of Australia</a> from 1990 to 1991.
</p><p>Keating was born in <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a> and left school at the age of 14. He joined the Labor Party at the same age, serving a term as State President of <a href="/wiki/Young_Labor" class="mw-redirect" title="Young Labor">Young Labor</a> and working as a research assistant for a trade union. He was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> at the age of 25, winning the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Blaxland" title="Division of Blaxland">division of Blaxland</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1969_Australian_federal_election" title="1969 Australian federal election">1969 election</a>. Keating briefly was <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Territories_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Territories (Australia)">Minister for Northern Australia</a> from October to November 1975, in the final weeks of the <a href="/wiki/Whitlam_government" title="Whitlam government">Whitlam government</a>. After <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">the Dismissal</a> removed Labor from power, he held senior portfolios in the <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet of Australia">Shadow Cabinets</a> of <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bill_Hayden" title="Bill Hayden">Bill Hayden</a>. During this time he came to be seen as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Labor_Right" title="Labor Right">Labor Right</a> faction, and developed a reputation as a talented and fierce parliamentary performer.
</p><p>After Labor's landslide victory at the <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">1983 election</a>, Keating was appointed treasurer by prime minister <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>. The pair developed a powerful political partnership, overseeing significant reforms intended to <a href="/wiki/Economic_liberalism" title="Economic liberalism">liberalise</a> and strengthen the Australian economy. These included the <a href="/wiki/Prices_and_Incomes_Accord" title="Prices and Incomes Accord">Prices and Incomes Accord</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Floating_exchange_rate" title="Floating exchange rate">float</a> of the Australian dollar, the elimination of <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a>, the deregulation of the financial sector, achieving the first federal <a href="/wiki/Budget_surplus" class="mw-redirect" title="Budget surplus">budget surplus</a> in Australian history, and reform of the <a href="/wiki/Taxation_in_Australia" title="Taxation in Australia">taxation system</a>, including the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Capital_gains_tax_in_Australia" title="Capital gains tax in Australia">capital gains tax</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fringe_benefits_tax_(Australia)" title="Fringe benefits tax (Australia)">fringe benefits tax</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Dividend_imputation" title="Dividend imputation">dividend imputation</a>. He also became recognised for his sardonic rhetoric, as a controversial but deeply skilled orator.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Keating became deputy prime minister in 1990, but in June 1991 he resigned from the Government to <a href="/wiki/June_1991_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="June 1991 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">unsuccessfully challenge</a> Hawke for the leadership, believing he had reneged on the <a href="/wiki/Kirribilli_Agreement_of_1988" title="Kirribilli Agreement of 1988">Kirribilli Agreement</a>. He mounted <a href="/wiki/December_1991_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="December 1991 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">a second successful challenge</a> six months later, and became prime minister.
</p><p>Keating was appointed prime minister in the aftermath of <a href="/wiki/Early_1990s_recession_in_Australia" title="Early 1990s recession in Australia">the early 1990s economic downturn</a>, which he had famously described as "the recession we had to have". This, combined with poor opinion polling, led many to predict Labor was certain to lose the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 election</a>, but Keating's government was re-elected in an <a href="/wiki/Upset_(competition)" title="Upset (competition)">upset victory</a>. In its second term, the <a href="/wiki/Keating_government" title="Keating government">Keating government</a> enacted the landmark <a href="/wiki/Native_Title_Act_1993" title="Native Title Act 1993"><i>Native Title Act</i></a> to enshrine <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_land_rights_in_Australia" title="Indigenous land rights in Australia">Indigenous land rights</a>, introduced <a href="/wiki/Superannuation_in_Australia" title="Superannuation in Australia">compulsory superannuation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Enterprise_bargaining" class="mw-redirect" title="Enterprise bargaining">enterprise bargaining</a>, created <a href="/wiki/One_Nation_(infrastructure)" title="One Nation (infrastructure)">a national infrastructure development program</a>, privatised <a href="/wiki/Qantas" title="Qantas">Qantas</a>, <a href="/wiki/CSL_Limited" title="CSL Limited">Commonwealth Serum Laboratories</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Bank" title="Commonwealth Bank">Commonwealth Bank</a>, established the <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">APEC</a> leaders' meeting, and promoted <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">republicanism</a> by establishing the <a href="/wiki/Republic_Advisory_Committee" title="Republic Advisory Committee">Republic Advisory Committee</a>.
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 election</a>, after 13 years in office, his government suffered a landslide defeat to the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal–National Coalition</a>, led by <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a>. Keating resigned as leader of the Labor Party and retired from Parliament shortly after the election, with his deputy <a href="/wiki/Kim_Beazley" title="Kim Beazley">Kim Beazley</a> being <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_election" title="1996 Australian Labor Party leadership election">elected unopposed</a> to replace him. Keating has since remained active as a political commentator, whilst maintaining a broad series of business interests, including serving on the international board of the <a href="/wiki/China_Development_Bank" title="China Development Bank">China Development Bank</a> from 2005 to 2018. 
</p><p>As prime minister, Keating performed poorly in <a href="/wiki/Opinion_poll" title="Opinion poll">opinion polls</a>, and in August 1993, received the lowest approval rating for any Australian prime minister since modern political polling began.<sup id="cite_ref-newspoll.com.au_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newspoll.com.au-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Since leaving office, Keating received broad praise from historians and commentators for his role in modernising the Australian economy as treasurer, although <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Australia">ratings of his premiership have been mixed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-age1_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-age1-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-afr_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-afr-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Keating has been recognised across the political spectrum for his charisma, debating skills, and his willingness to boldly confront social norms,<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> including his famous <a href="/wiki/Redfern_Park_Speech" title="Redfern Park Speech">Redfern Park Speech</a> on the impact of <a href="/wiki/History_of_Australia_(1788%E2%80%931850)" title="History of Australia (1788–1850)">colonisation in Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_reconciliation" class="mw-redirect" title="Aboriginal reconciliation">Aboriginal reconciliation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Keating was born at <a href="/wiki/St_Margaret%27s_Hospital,_Sydney" title="St Margaret&#39;s Hospital, Sydney">St Margaret's Hospital</a> in <a href="/wiki/Darlinghurst" title="Darlinghurst">Darlinghurst</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a>, on 18 January 1944.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He was the first of four children born to Minnie (née Chapman) and Matthew John Keating. His father worked as a <a href="/wiki/Boilermaker" title="Boilermaker">boilermaker</a> for the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Government_Railways" title="New South Wales Government Railways">New South Wales Government Railways</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> All of Keating's grandparents were born in Australia. On his father's side, he was descended from Irish immigrants born in counties <a href="/wiki/County_Galway" title="County Galway">Galway</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Roscommon" title="County Roscommon">Roscommon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/County_Tipperary" title="County Tipperary">Tipperary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> On his mother's side, he was of mixed English and Irish descent. His maternal grandfather, Fred Chapman, was the son of two <a href="/wiki/Convicts_in_Australia" title="Convicts in Australia">convicts</a>, John Chapman and Sarah Gallagher, both of whom had been <a href="/wiki/Penal_transportation" title="Penal transportation">transported</a> for theft in the 1830s.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating grew up in <a href="/wiki/Bankstown" title="Bankstown">Bankstown</a>, a working-class suburb in western Sydney, with the family home from 1942 to 1966 being a modest <a href="/wiki/Asbestos_cement" title="Asbestos cement">fibro</a> and brick bungalow at 3 Marshall Street, Bankstown (demolished for flat development in 2014).<sup id="cite_ref-RE_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RE-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> His siblings include Anne Keating, a company director and businesswoman. Leaving De La Salle College—now known as <a href="/wiki/LaSalle_Catholic_College,_Bankstown" title="LaSalle Catholic College, Bankstown">LaSalle Catholic College</a>—at the age of 14, Keating left high school rather than pursuing higher education, instead working as a pay clerk at the <a href="/wiki/Sydney_County_Council" title="Sydney County Council">Sydney County Council</a>'s electricity distributor. Keating also attended Belmore Technical High School to further his education.<sup id="cite_ref-naa_before_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa_before-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He then worked as research assistant for a trade union, having joined the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor Party</a> as soon as he was eligible. In 1966, he became president of New South Wales Young Labor.<sup id="cite_ref-bio_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bio-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> During the 1960s, Keating also managed a rock band named The Ramrods.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_Keating_1970.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Paul_Keating_1970.png/170px-Paul_Keating_1970.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Paul_Keating_1970.png/255px-Paul_Keating_1970.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Paul_Keating_1970.png/340px-Paul_Keating_1970.png 2x" data-file-width="440" data-file-height="550" /></a><figcaption>Keating in 1970, shortly after he was first elected to Parliament</figcaption></figure>
<p>Through his contacts in the unions and Young Labor, then known as Youth Council, Keating met future senior Labor figures such as <a href="/wiki/Laurie_Brereton" title="Laurie Brereton">Laurie Brereton</a>, <a href="/wiki/Graham_Richardson" title="Graham Richardson">Graham Richardson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>. He also developed a friendship with former <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">New South Wales Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Jack_Lang_(Australian_politician)" title="Jack Lang (Australian politician)">Jack Lang</a>, who Keating took on as a political mentor. In 1971, he succeeded in having Lang re-admitted to the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Keating successfully gained the Labor nomination for the seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Blaxland" title="Division of Blaxland">Blaxland</a> in the western suburbs of Sydney, and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> in <a href="/wiki/1969_Australian_federal_election" title="1969 Australian federal election">1969</a> when he was just 25 years old.<sup id="cite_ref-bio_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bio-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating was initially more <a href="/wiki/Socially_conservative" class="mw-redirect" title="Socially conservative">socially conservative</a>; in his <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> he declared that the Liberal government had "boasted about the increasing number of women in the workforce. Rather than something to be proud of, I feel it is something of which we should be ashamed".<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He later voted against former prime minister <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a>'s motion to decriminalise <a href="/wiki/Homosexuality" title="Homosexuality">homosexuality</a> in 1973. According to <a href="/wiki/Tom_Uren" title="Tom Uren">Tom Uren</a> he was originally a "very narrow-minded young man", who later "matured" and became far less socially conservative.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jamoe.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Jamoe.jpg/220px-Jamoe.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Jamoe.jpg/330px-Jamoe.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Jamoe.jpg/440px-Jamoe.jpg 2x" data-file-width="875" data-file-height="618" /></a><figcaption>Keating aged 34, second from left, with Labor figures (from left) <a href="/wiki/Colin_Jamieson" title="Colin Jamieson">Colin Jamieson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Walsh_(Australian_politician)" title="Peter Walsh (Australian politician)">Peter Walsh</a> and <a href="/wiki/Stewart_West" title="Stewart West">Stewart West</a> in <a href="/wiki/Wickham,_Western_Australia" title="Wickham, Western Australia">Wickham</a>, 1978</figcaption></figure>
<p>After Labor's victory at the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 election</a>, Keating narrowly failed to be elected to serve in the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">Cabinet</a>, instead being a backbencher for most of the <a href="/wiki/Whitlam_government" title="Whitlam government">Whitlam government</a>. He was eventually appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Territories_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Territories (Australia)">Minister for Northern Australia</a> in October 1975, but served in the role only until the Government was <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">controversially dismissed</a> by Governor-General <a href="/wiki/John_Kerr_(governor-general)" title="John Kerr (governor-general)">John Kerr</a> the following month. In a 2013 interview with <a href="/wiki/Kerry_O%27Brien_(journalist)" title="Kerry O&#39;Brien (journalist)">Kerry O'Brien</a>, Keating called the dismissal a "coup" and raised the idea to "arrest [Kerr]" and "lock him up", adding that he would not have "[taken] it lying down" if he was prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After Labor's defeat in the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">1975 election</a>, Keating was quickly added to the Shadow Cabinet, serving as Shadow Minister for Minerals, Resources and Energy until January 1983.<sup id="cite_ref-naa_before_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa_before-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> During this time he achieved a reputation as a flamboyant and fierce parliamentary performer, adopting the style of an aggressive debater. In 1981, he was elected president of the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Labor_Party" title="New South Wales Labor Party">New South Wales Labor Party</a>, thus becoming the leader of the influential <a href="/wiki/Labor_Right" title="Labor Right">Labor Right</a> faction. At this time, he initially supported the former <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> <a href="/wiki/Bill_Hayden" title="Bill Hayden">Bill Hayden</a> for Labor Leader over the former <a href="/wiki/Australian_Council_of_Trade_Unions" title="Australian Council of Trade Unions">ACTU President</a> <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> as leadership tensions between the two men began to mount; he later explained that part of his reasoning was that he privately hoped to succeed Hayden himself in the near future.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> However, by 1982, the members of his faction had swung behind Hawke, and Keating endorsed his challenge. The formal announcement of Keating's support for Hawke was written by a fellow Labor politician, <a href="/wiki/Gareth_Evans_(politician)" title="Gareth Evans (politician)">Gareth Evans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.159_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.159-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Hayden survived the challenge, pressure continued to mount on him. In an attempt to shore up his position, Hayden promoted Keating to the role of Shadow Treasurer in January 1983. However this did not prove sufficient and Hayden resigned a month later, after a <a href="/wiki/1982_Flinders_by-election" title="1982 Flinders by-election">poor by-election result in the federal electorate of Flinders in Victoria</a>. Hawke was elected unopposed to replace him and Hawke subsequently led Labor to a landslide victory in the <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">1983 election</a> just six weeks later.<sup id="cite_ref-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.159_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.159-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Treasurer_of_Australia">Treasurer of Australia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Treasurer of Australia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Hawke_government" title="Hawke government">Hawke government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_days">Early days</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early days">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following Labor's victory in the 1983 election, Keating was appointed <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer of Australia</a> by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>; he succeeded <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> in the position.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> He and Hawke were able to use the size of the budget deficit that the <a href="/wiki/Hawke_government" title="Hawke government">Hawke government</a> had inherited from the <a href="/wiki/Fraser_government" title="Fraser government">Fraser government</a> to question the economic credibility of the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National Coalition</a> over the coming years.<sup id="cite_ref-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.148_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.148-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> According to Hawke, the historically large $9.6&#160;billion budget deficit left by the Coalition "became a stick with which we were justifiably able to beat the Opposition".<sup id="cite_ref-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.148_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.148-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Although Howard was widely regarded at this time as being "discredited" by the hidden deficit, he had in fact argued unsuccessfully against Fraser that the revised figures should be disclosed before the election.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the ensuing years, Hawke and Keating developed an extremely powerful partnership, which proved to be essential to Labor's success in government; multiple Labor figures in years since have cited the partnership between the two as the party's greatest ever.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The two men proved a study in contrasts: Hawke was a Rhodes Scholar; Keating left high school early.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Hawke's enthusiasms were cigars, betting and most forms of sport; Keating preferred <a href="/wiki/Classical_architecture" title="Classical architecture">classical architecture</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gustav_Mahler" title="Gustav Mahler">Mahler</a> symphonies and collecting <a href="/wiki/British_Regency" class="mw-redirect" title="British Regency">British Regency</a> and <a href="/wiki/First_French_Empire" title="First French Empire">French Empire</a> antiques.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Despite not knowing one another before Hawke assumed the leadership in 1983, the two formed a personal as well as political relationship which enabled the Government to pursue a significant number of reforms, although there were occasional points of tension between the two.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating, along with Hawke, oversaw a "National Economic Summit" in their first month in office, with Keating leading several sessions outlining the Government's economic agenda. The Summit, which brought together a significant number of senior business and industrial figures alongside trade union leaders and politicians, led to a unanimous adoption of a national economic strategy, generating sufficient political capital for the Government to begin a wide-ranging programme of economic reform previously resisted by much of the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Macroeconomic_reforms">Macroeconomic reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Macroeconomic reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><span><video id="mwe_player_0" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/220px--ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="220" height="124" data-durationhint="116" data-mwtitle="ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons" resource="/wiki/File:ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv" type="video/ogg; codecs=&quot;theora, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file, 512 × 288 (1.11 Mbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="512" data-height="288" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.120p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Lowest bandwidth VP9 (120P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 120P" data-transcodekey="120p.vp9.webm" data-width="214" data-height="120" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.160p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth WebM (160P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 160P" data-transcodekey="160p.webm" data-width="284" data-height="160" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.180p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="320" data-height="180" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Small VP9 (240P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.vp9.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv/ABC_Dollar_Float.ogv.240p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Small WebM (240P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /><track src="https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/api.php?action=timedtext&amp;title=File%3AABC_Dollar_Float.ogv&amp;lang=en&amp;trackformat=vtt&amp;origin=%2A" kind="subtitles" type="text/vtt" srclang="en" label="English ‪(en)‬" data-dir="ltr" /></video></span><figcaption><a href="/wiki/ABC_News_(Australia)" title="ABC News (Australia)">ABC News</a> report on the first day of trading with a floating Australian dollar</figcaption></figure>
<p>Keating used the authority and relative autonomy provided to him by Hawke to become one of the major driving forces behind the various extensive <a href="/wiki/Macroeconomic" class="mw-redirect" title="Macroeconomic">macroeconomic</a> reforms of the Government.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In December 1983, Hawke and Keating approved the <a href="/wiki/Floating_exchange_rate" title="Floating exchange rate">floating</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_dollar" title="Australian dollar">Australian dollar</a>, disregarding advice from the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_the_Treasury_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of the Treasury (Australia)">Treasury Secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Stone_(Australian_politician)" title="John Stone (Australian politician)">John Stone</a> to retain the fixed currency framework.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The success of the move, which was lauded by economic and media commentators, gave confidence to Keating to pursue even more reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Over the Hawke government's first and second terms, Keating oversaw the gradual elimination of <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> on imports,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> the privatisation of several state-owned companies such as <a href="/wiki/Qantas" title="Qantas">Qantas</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/CSL_Limited" title="CSL Limited">CSL Limited</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Bank" title="Commonwealth Bank">Commonwealth Bank</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> the deregulation of significant sections of the banking system (including allowing for numerous foreign-owned banks to begin operating in Australia for the first time) and the granting of autonomy on decision-making to the <a href="/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_Australia" title="Reserve Bank of Australia">Reserve Bank of Australia</a>.
</p><p>Keating was also instrumental in establishing the Hawke government's signature industrial relations and wages policy, the <a href="/wiki/Prices_and_Incomes_Accord" title="Prices and Incomes Accord">Prices and Incomes Accord</a>. This was an agreement directly between the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Council_of_Trade_Unions" title="Australian Council of Trade Unions">Australian Council of Trade Unions</a> (ACTU) and the Government to guarantee a reduction in demands for wage increases, in exchange for the Government providing a significant increase in social programmes, including the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Australia)" title="Medicare (Australia)">Medicare</a> and the Family Assistance Scheme; in so doing, the Government was able to reduce inflation and unemployment over the decade.<sup id="cite_ref-Ryan_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryan-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Keating's management of the Accord, and the close working relationship he developed with ACTU Secretary <a href="/wiki/Bill_Kelty" title="Bill Kelty">Bill Kelty</a>, became a source of significant political power for Keating, who negotiated multiple versions of the Accord with Kelty throughout the Hawke government. Through the power given to him, Keating was often able to bypass the Cabinet altogether, notably in exercising monetary policy, and he was regularly referred to as "the most powerful Treasurer in modern times".<sup id="cite_ref-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the wake of the raft of macroeconomic reforms introduced by Keating throughout the first term of the Hawke government in particular, in 1984 he was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Euromoney_Finance_Minister_of_the_Year" title="Euromoney Finance Minister of the Year">Euromoney Finance Minister of the Year</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> an award which became colloquially known in Australia as the "World's Greatest Treasurer", becoming the first Australian Treasurer to be presented with the award.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Microeconomic_reforms">Microeconomic reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Microeconomic reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg/170px-Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg/255px-Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg/340px-Jean-Claude_Paye_and_Paul_Keating.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="514" /></a><figcaption>Keating with <a href="/wiki/OECD" title="OECD">OECD</a> Secretary-General <a href="/wiki/Jean-Claude_Paye_(OECD)" title="Jean-Claude Paye (OECD)">Jean-Claude Paye</a>, 1985</figcaption></figure>
<p>Throughout the second and third terms of the Hawke government, Keating led a significant overhaul of the long-stagnant <a href="/wiki/Australian_taxation" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian taxation">Australian taxation system</a>. In 1985, he became a passionate advocate within the Cabinet for the introduction of a <a href="/wiki/Consumption_tax" title="Consumption tax">broad-based consumption tax</a>, similar in nature to the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">goods and services tax</a> that was later introduced by the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a>, as a means of addressing Australia's chronic balance of payments issue.<sup id="cite_ref-Eccleston_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eccleston-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Malone_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Malone-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In the build-up to the <a href="/wiki/1984_Australian_federal_election" title="1984 Australian federal election">1984 election</a>, Hawke promised a policy paper on taxation reform to be discussed with all stakeholders at a "National Taxation Summit". Three options&#160;– A, B and C&#160;– were presented in the paper, with Keating and his Treasury colleagues fiercely advocating for C, which included a consumption tax of 15% on goods and services along with reductions in personal and company income tax, a fringe benefits tax and a capital gains tax. Although Keating was able to win the support of a reluctant Cabinet, Hawke believed that the opposition from the public, the ACTU, and the business community would be too great. He therefore decided to abandon any plans for a consumption tax, although the remainder of the reforms were adopted in the tax reform package. The loss of the consumption tax was seen a defeat for Keating; he later joked about it at a press conference, saying, "It's a bit like <i>Ben Hur</i>. We've crossed the line with one wheel off, but we have crossed the line."<sup id="cite_ref-DAlpuget_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DAlpuget-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Whilst the remainder of the package represented the biggest overhaul of the Australian taxation system for decades, Keating continued to agitate for further changes to address the balance of payments problems faced by Australia. On 14 May 1986, frustrated at the slow pace of dealing with the issue, Keating caused considerable public comment and a degree of controversy when he declared on a radio programme that if Australia did not address the problem, it risked degenerating to the status of a "<a href="/wiki/Banana_republic" title="Banana republic">banana republic</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-Guardian_papers_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guardian_papers-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Although the remark was quickly disowned by Hawke in public, the Government increased efforts to deal with the growing balance of payments crisis. With no consumption tax to generate a significant increase in incomings, Keating and his ministerial colleagues led a process to significantly reduce Government outlays instead, resulting in some criticism from the grassroots of the Labor Party, who opposed cuts to spending.<sup id="cite_ref-Guardian_papers_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guardian_papers-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Despite the criticism, the Government was able to produce a national <a href="/wiki/Budget_surplus" class="mw-redirect" title="Budget surplus">budget surplus</a> for the years 1988, 1989 and 1990, with the surplus of 1988 proving to be the largest budget surplus in Australian history.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the campaign for the <a href="/wiki/1987_Australian_federal_election" title="1987 Australian federal election">1987 election</a>, Keating was credited as dealing a "fatal" blow to the Liberal-National Coalition's hopes for victory, after giving a press conference in which he exposed a significant accounting error in the costings the Liberal Party had released to demonstrate how its economic policies would be paid for.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Then-Opposition Leader John Howard accepted the error, and subsequent opinion polling reported that the mistake greatly contributed to Labor's vote in what proved to be a landslide victory.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating's later tenure as treasurer was heavily criticised by some for consistently high interest rates, which Keating argued was necessary to reduce economic growth gradually so that demand for imports did not grow out of control. Throughout the 1980s, both the global and Australian economies grew quickly, and by the late 1980s, inflation had grown to around 9%. By 1988, the Reserve Bank of Australia began tightening monetary policy, and household interest rates peaked at 18%. It is often said that the bank was too slow in easing monetary policy, and that this ultimately led to a recession. In private, Keating had argued for rates to rise earlier than they did, and fall sooner, although his view was at odds with the Reserve Bank and his Treasury colleagues.<sup id="cite_ref-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Publicly, Hawke and Keating had said there would be no recession&#160;– or that there would be a "soft landing"&#160;– but this changed when Keating announced the country was indeed in recession in 1990, several months after the Hawke government had won an unprecedented fourth consecutive term in office. Announcing the recession, Keating memorably stated that the recession was a "recession Australia had to have". The remark was referred to by political journalist <a href="/wiki/Paul_Kelly_(journalist)" title="Paul Kelly (journalist)">Paul Kelly</a> as "perhaps the most stupid remark of Keating's career, and it nearly cost him the Prime Ministership." Kelly did also concede that, "...however, it is largely true that the boom begat the recession."<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The economic reform package throughout the 1980s has been claimed by numerous economic commentators and journalists to have been the basis for an unprecedentedly long period of economic growth, with Australia's <a href="/wiki/Gross_domestic_product" title="Gross domestic product">gross domestic product</a> increasing unbroken every year for 30 years, and the end of chronic inflation and balance of payments difficulties, along with the increasingly globalised domestic economy, enabling long periods of stability and growth.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_challenges">Leadership challenges</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Leadership challenges">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At the end of 1988, Keating, who had long believed that he would succeed Hawke as prime minister, began to put pressure on Hawke to retire in the new year. Hawke rejected this advice, but reached a <a href="/wiki/Kirribilli_Agreement_of_1988" title="Kirribilli Agreement of 1988">secret agreement</a> with Keating that he would remain as leader through to the <a href="/wiki/1990_Australian_federal_election" title="1990 Australian federal election">1990 election</a>, and that he would resign in Keating's favour shortly after the election, which he convinced Keating he could win.<sup id="cite_ref-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994_43-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PKelly_EndOfCertainty1994-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Hawke subsequently won that election, albeit narrowly, and appointed Keating his <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Deputy Prime Minister of Australia">deputy prime minister</a> to replace the retiring <a href="/wiki/Lionel_Bowen" title="Lionel Bowen">Lionel Bowen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> However, by the end of 1990, frustrated at the lack of any indication from Hawke as to when he might retire, Keating delivered a provocative speech questioning the direction of the government. As a result, Hawke told Keating he would renege on the deal on the basis that Keating had been publicly disloyal.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although tensions between the two remained private for some time, Keating eventually resigned from the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">Cabinet</a> in June 1991 and <a href="/wiki/June_1991_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="June 1991 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">challenged for the leadership</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Hawke won the ballot by 66 votes to 44,<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> and in a press statement afterwards Keating declared that he had fired his "one shot" as regards the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Publicly, at least, this seemed to spell the end of his leadership ambitions. Having failed to defeat Hawke, Keating realised that events would have to move very much in his favour for a second challenge to be even possible, and he strongly considered retiring from politics altogether.<sup id="cite_ref-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.439_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Edwards,_John_1996,_p.439-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> However, Hawke's leadership was regarded by many as being "wounded" as a result of losing his long-term political partner and the growing confidence of the Liberal-National Coalition under the new leadership of <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.544_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hawke,_Bob_1994,_p.544-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> After Hawke was forced to sack <a href="/wiki/John_Kerin" title="John Kerin">John Kerin</a>, the man appointed to replace Keating as treasurer, for a public gaffe in attempting to combat the Coalition's new '<a href="/wiki/Fightback!_(policy)" title="Fightback! (policy)">Fightback!</a>' policy,<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Keating took the opportunity to <a href="/wiki/December_1991_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="December 1991 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">challenge a second time</a> in December 1991, this time emerging victorious by 56 votes to 51.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Keating paid tribute to Hawke's nine years as prime minister, and stated that he would provide a robust challenge to Hewson.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_.281991.E2.80.931996.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_(1991–1996)">Prime Minister of Australia (1991–1996)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Prime Minister of Australia (1991–1996)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Keating_government" title="Keating government">Keating government</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hawke%E2%80%93Keating_government" title="Hawke–Keating government">Hawke–Keating government</a></div>
<p>On 20 December 1991, Keating was sworn in as prime minister by the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> Bill Hayden. 
On becoming prime minister, Keating thought of becoming treasurer again, noting that state premiers had often been their own treasurers, but decided against it.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
John Dawkins was appointed treasurer instead.
</p><p>Keating entered office with an extensive legislative agenda, including pursuing reconciliation with Australia's <a href="/wiki/Australian_Aborigine" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Aborigine">Indigenous population</a>, deepening Australia's economic and cultural ties with Asia, and <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">making Australia a republic</a>. The addressing of these issues came to be known as Keating's "big picture".<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Indigenous_land_rights_and_domestic_policy">Indigenous land rights and domestic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Indigenous land rights and domestic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><span><video id="mwe_player_1" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm/220px--Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="220" height="124" data-durationhint="106" data-mwtitle="Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons" resource="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm" type="video/webm;
