codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Lowest bandwidth VP9 (120P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 120P" data-transcodekey="120p.vp9.webm" data-width="214" data-height="120" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm.160p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth WebM (160P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 160P" data-transcodekey="160p.webm" data-width="284" data-height="160" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm.180p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="320" data-height="180" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Small VP9 (240P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.vp9.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/8f/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm/Prime_Minister_Paul_Keating_visits_Indonesia_ABC_1992.webm.240p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Small WebM (240P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /></video></span><figcaption><a href="/wiki/ABC_News_(Australia)" title="ABC News (Australia)">ABC News</a> footage of Keating visiting <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a> in 1992 and meeting with Indonesian President <a href="/wiki/Suharto" title="Suharto">Suharto</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Shortly after Keating became prime minister, the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court of Australia</a> handed down a <a href="/wiki/Mabo_v_Queensland_(No_2)" title="Mabo v Queensland (No 2)">judgment</a> in a long-running case on <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_land_rights_in_Australia" title="Indigenous land rights in Australia">Indigenous land rights</a>; the judgement would come to be known as <i>Mabo</i>, and declared that a right to <a href="/wiki/Native_title_in_Australia" title="Native title in Australia">native title</a> did exist in Australia, overturning <a href="/wiki/Terra_nullius" title="Terra nullius">terra nullius</a>, but not clarifying exactly who had the right to access the title.<sup id="cite_ref-Mabo_#2_HCA_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mabo_#2_HCA-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Keating led the Government's response to the ruling, beginning a high-profile public campaign on raising awareness of the issue, and advocating repeatedly in favour of the judgment and for an expansion of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander land rights. On 10 December 1992, Keating delivered a major address which came to be known as the <a href="/wiki/Redfern_Park_Speech" title="Redfern Park Speech">Redfern Park Speech</a> on <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_reconciliation" class="mw-redirect" title="Aboriginal reconciliation">Aboriginal reconciliation</a>, a speech which has since regularly been cited as among the greatest in Australian political history, in which he explicitly noted the responsibility of settler Australians for destroying much of Indigenous society.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This work culminated in the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Native_Title_Act_1993" title="Native Title Act 1993">Native Title Act</a> in 1993, which "provide(d) a national system for the recognition and protection of <a href="/wiki/Native_title_in_Australia" title="Native title in Australia">native title</a> and for its co-existence with the national land management system".<sup id="cite_ref-Mabo_#2_HCA_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mabo_#2_HCA-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> As well as creating the legal field of native title, the Act established an <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_and_Torres_Strait_Islander_Social_Justice_Commissioner" class="mw-redirect" title="Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Social Justice Commissioner">Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Social Justice Commissioner</a>, who was required to prepare an annual report to the <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_for_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Attorney-General for Australia">Attorney-General</a> on the operation of the Native Title Act and its effect on the exercise and enjoyment of human rights of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples, and to report, when requested by the attorney-general, on any other matter relating to the rights of Indigenous people under the Act.<sup id="cite_ref-ahrcnt_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ahrcnt-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park,_1992.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Paul_Keating_delivering_the_Redfern_Speech_at_Redfern_Park%2C_1992.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="8205" data-file-height="5574" /></a><figcaption>Keating delivering the <a href="/wiki/Redfern_Park_Speech" title="Redfern Park Speech">Redfern Park Speech</a> on 10 December 1992</figcaption></figure>
<p>Elsewhere in domestic policy, Keating established and promoted the first Commonwealth cultural policy, known as 'Creative Nation'.<sup id="cite_ref-creative_nation_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-creative_nation-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The policy allocated AU$250 million over four years to promote the cultural and creative arts sectors in Australia. Keating had frequently espoused the benefits of the arts in public, and used the policy as an opportunity to develop the Australian cultural sector.<sup id="cite_ref-creative_nation_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-creative_nation-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> During the Keating government, <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_detention_in_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Mandatory detention in Australia">mandatory detention for asylum seekers</a> was also introduced for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-SBS_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SBS-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Superannuation_and_economic_policy">Superannuation and economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Superannuation and economic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg/220px-Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg/330px-Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg/440px-Second_Keating_Cabinet_1994.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3937" data-file-height="2788" /></a><figcaption>Keating and his Cabinet, 1994</figcaption></figure>
<p>Arguably Keating's most far-reaching achievement as prime minister was the full introduction of the <a href="/wiki/Superannuation_in_Australia" title="Superannuation in Australia">National Superannuation Scheme</a>, implemented to address Australia's long-term problem of chronically low national savings. This initiative built on policies that Keating had pursued whilst treasurer, and was aimed at ensuring that most Australians would have enough money to retire. In 1992, the compulsory employer contribution scheme became a part of a wider reform package addressing this retirement income dilemma. It had been demonstrated that Australia, along with many other Western nations, would experience a major <a href="/wiki/Population_ageing" title="Population ageing">demographic shift</a> in the coming decades, due to ageing population, and it was claimed that this would result in increased pension payments that would place an unaffordable strain on the <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Australia" title="Economy of Australia">Australian economy</a>.
</p><p>Keating's solution was a "three pillars" approach to retirement income, requiring compulsory employer contributions to superannuation funds, permitting further contributions to superannuation funds and other investments, and introducing, where this was insufficient, a safety net consisting of a means-tested government-funded age pension.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The compulsory employer contributions were branded "Superannuation Guarantee" (SG) contributions.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> As a result of this policy, along with the gradual increases in the minimum contribution amount, Australia grew to become the fourth largest holder of pension fund assets in the world, with a balance of AU$3.3 trillion in superannuation assets at the end of the June 2022 quarter.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the aftermath of the 1990 recession, Keating appointed his close ally <a href="/wiki/John_Dawkins" title="John Dawkins">John Dawkins</a> as treasurer, and together the two developed an economic package to counter the Liberal-National Coalition's '<a href="/wiki/Fightback!_(policy)" title="Fightback! (policy)">Fightback!</a>' proposals; this package came to be known as 'One Nation', and involved using funding from the budget surplus to produce new welfare-to-work programmes, as well as introducing a new degree of competition within the telecommunications and communications industries and creating the Australian National Training Authority (ANTA).<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_papers_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_papers-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> 'One Nation' also proposed a series of further tax cuts for middle-income workers coming in two tranches, in 1993 and 1995, although these would later be deferred to 1995 and 1998, a move which cost the Government considerable political support among the public.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_papers_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_papers-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> A further major economic policy development was the introduction of an <a href="/wiki/Enterprise_bargaining" class="mw-redirect" title="Enterprise bargaining">enterprise bargaining</a> scheme as part of the final stage of the <a href="/wiki/Prices_and_Incomes_Accord" title="Prices and Incomes Accord">Prices and Incomes Accord</a>, intended to allow for greater flexibility and economies of scale within industrial wage arbitration, although much of this was curtailed by the Howard government after 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_papers_80-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_papers-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Australia_and_the_Indonesian_occupation_of_East_Timor#Keating_era_Keating_era_1994–98" title="Australia and the Indonesian occupation of East Timor">Australia and the Indonesian occupation of East Timor §&#160;Keating era Keating era 1994–98</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Clinton_Keating.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Clinton_Keating.jpg/170px-Clinton_Keating.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="246" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Clinton_Keating.jpg/255px-Clinton_Keating.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/73/Clinton_Keating.jpg 2x" data-file-width="294" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Keating with <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">U.S. President</a> <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a> (left) in 1993</figcaption></figure>
<p>Throughout his time as prime minister, Keating took a number of steps to strengthen and develop bilateral links with Australia's closest neighbours; he frequently said that there was no country in the world that was more important to Australia than <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>, and undertook his first overseas visit to the country, becoming the first Australian prime minister to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Keating made a conscious effort to develop a personal relationship with <a href="/wiki/President_of_Indonesia" title="President of Indonesia">Indonesian President</a> <a href="/wiki/Suharto" title="Suharto">Suharto</a>, and to include Indonesia in multilateral forums attended by Australia. Keating's friendship with Suharto was criticised by human rights activists supportive of <a href="/wiki/East_Timor" title="East Timor">East Timorese</a> independence, and by <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> winner <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ramos-Horta" title="José Ramos-Horta">José Ramos-Horta</a>. The Keating government's cooperation with the Indonesian military, and the signing of the <a href="/wiki/Timor_Gap_Treaty" title="Timor Gap Treaty">Timor Gap Treaty</a>, were also strongly criticised by these same groups. It was alleged by some that Keating was overlooking alleged human rights abuses by the Indonesian government as part of his effort to dramatically increase Australia's cultural, diplomatic and economic ties with Asia.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Following the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation</a> (APEC) Economic Forum by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>, Keating developed the idea further, winning the support in 1993 of recently elected <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">US President</a> <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China" title="Premier of the People&#39;s Republic of China">Chinese Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Li_Peng" title="Li Peng">Li Peng</a> to expand APEC to a full Leaders' Meeting. This led to APEC becoming one of the most significant high-level international summits, and at the 1994 APEC Leaders' Meeting, hosted by Indonesia, members agreed to the Keating government's proposals for what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Bogor" title="Bogor">Bogor Declaration</a>, which set targets for a significant increase in free trade and investment between industrialised APEC countries by 2010 and between developing APEC countries by 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-pecc.org_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pecc.org-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In December 1993, Keating became involved in a diplomatic incident with <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a> when he described Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Mahathir_bin_Mohamad" class="mw-redirect" title="Mahathir bin Mohamad">Mahathir Mohamad</a> as "recalcitrant". The incident occurred after Mahathir refused to attend the 1993 <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">APEC summit</a>. Keating said "APEC is bigger than all of us&#160;– Australia, the U.S. and Malaysia, and Dr. Mahathir and any other recalcitrants." The translation of the word "recalcitrant" into Malaysian rendered the word a more egregious insult, and Mahathir demanded an apology from Keating, threatening to reduce diplomatic ties and trade drastically with Australia, which became an enormous concern to Australian exporters. Some Malaysian officials talked of launching a "Buy Australian Last" campaign; Keating subsequently apologised to Mahathir over the remark.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_recalcitrant_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT_recalcitrant-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1993_and_1996_elections">1993 and 1996 elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 1993 and 1996 elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:PaulKeating1993.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/PaulKeating1993.jpg/170px-PaulKeating1993.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0a/PaulKeating1993.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="220" data-file-height="312" /></a><figcaption>Keating at the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> in 1993</figcaption></figure>
<p>As prime minister, Keating maintained his aggressive debating style. When asked by <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Opposition Leader</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> why he would not call an early election, Keating replied, "because I want to do you slowly." He referred to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Liberal_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Liberal Party">Liberal Party</a> as "a motley, dishonest crew", and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian National Party">National Party</a> as "dummies and dimwits; desperadoes". During an opposition debate that sought to censure Keating, he described being attacked by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Costello" title="Peter Costello">Peter Costello</a> as "like being flogged with warm lettuce". Despite this renewed attack on the Opposition, and a busy legislative agenda, many commentators predicted that the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 election</a> was "unwinnable" for Labor.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the campaign, Keating focused a great amount of effort on attacking the Coalition's proposed <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">goods and services tax</a> (GST), arguing that it would prove "a dead weight" on the economy, and stating that "every time you put your hand in your pocket, <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">Dr. Hewson's</a> will be in there with you". He was helped by Hewson struggling towards the end of the campaign to <a href="/wiki/Birthday_cake_interview" title="Birthday cake interview">explain exactly which products</a> would have the GST levied on them, and which would not. Having begun the campaign far behind the Coalition in opinion polls, on 13 March Keating led Labor to an unexpected and record-breaking <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">fifth consecutive election victory</a>, picking up a two-seat swing. The speech Keating delivered at the victory celebration has been described as one of the great Labor speeches.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Opening with "This is a victory for the true believers; the men and women of Australia who, in difficult times, have kept the faith", the speech has been described as providing a source of inspiration for Labor Party faithful to the present day.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like Hawke before him, Keating was able to benefit from disunity in the Liberal Party. Fourteen months after the March 1993 election, John Hewson was replaced as Liberal Leader by <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Downer" title="Alexander Downer">Alexander Downer</a>, whose leadership was quickly marred by gaffes and controversies within months. Keating routinely succeeded in outwitting Downer within Parliament, and in early 1995, Downer resigned to be replaced by <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a>, who had previously led the Liberals from 1985 to 1989. Howard was able to give the Coalition renewed momentum after Labor lost the seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Canberra" title="Division of Canberra">Canberra</a> in a <a href="/wiki/1995_Canberra_by-election" title="1995 Canberra by-election">by-election</a>. In contrast to Hewson, Howard adopted a "small target" campaign strategy for the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 election</a>, publicly committing to keep numerous Labor reforms such as <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Australia)" title="Medicare (Australia)">Medicare</a>, and defusing the republic issue by promising to hold a <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(Australia)" title="Constitutional Convention (Australia)">constitutional convention</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> This combined with a narrative of "time for a change" led to a heavy defeat for the Keating government on 2 March 1996, suffering a five percent <a href="/wiki/Two_party_preferred" class="mw-redirect" title="Two party preferred">two party preferred</a> swing and losing 29 seats, making it the second-worst defeat of a sitting government in Australian history. Keating announced he would retire as Labor Leader and from Parliament, and tendered his resignation as prime minister on 11 March, 13 years to the day after <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> had first taken office.<sup id="cite_ref-naa_afteroffice_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa_afteroffice-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-political_career">Post-political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Post-political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_Keating_2007_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/85/Paul_Keating_2007_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="188" height="257" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="188" data-file-height="257" /></a><figcaption>Keating in 2007</figcaption></figure>
<p>After leaving Parliament in 1996, Keating moved to the affluent <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Suburbs_(Sydney)" title="Eastern Suburbs (Sydney)">eastern Sydney</a> suburb of <a href="/wiki/Woollahra" class="mw-redirect" title="Woollahra">Woollahra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> He accepted appointment as a director for various companies, and also became a senior adviser to <a href="/wiki/Lazard" title="Lazard">Lazard</a>, an investment banking firm.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Keating was also appointed to the advisory council to the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Chinese Government">Chinese Government Development Bank</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> He was also appointed a visiting professor of public policy at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a> and was awarded <a href="/wiki/Honorary_degree" title="Honorary degree">honorary doctorates in law</a> from <a href="/wiki/Keio_University" title="Keio University">Keio University</a> in Tokyo (1995), the <a href="/wiki/National_University_of_Singapore" title="National University of Singapore">National University of Singapore</a> (1999), the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a> (2003) and <a href="/wiki/Macquarie_University" title="Macquarie University">Macquarie University</a> (2012).<sup id="cite_ref-naa_afteroffice_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa_afteroffice-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In 1997, Keating declined appointment in the <a href="/wiki/1997_Australia_Day_Honours" title="1997 Australia Day Honours">Australia Day Honours</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a>, an honour which has been offered to all former prime ministers since the modern <a href="/wiki/Australian_Honours_System" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Honours System">Australian Honours System</a> was introduced in 1975.<sup id="cite_ref-naa_afteroffice_92-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa_afteroffice-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> On his refusal, Keating expressed that he had long believed honours should be reserved for those whose work in the community went unrecognised and that having been prime minister was sufficient public recognition.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2000, he published his first book since leaving office, <i>Engagement: Australia Faces the Asia-Pacific</i>, which focused on foreign policy during his time as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In 2002, Keating's former speechwriter and adviser, <a href="/wiki/Don_Watson" title="Don Watson">Don Watson</a>, published <i><a href="/wiki/Recollections_of_a_Bleeding_Heart:_A_Portrait_of_Paul_Keating_PM" class="mw-redirect" title="Recollections of a Bleeding Heart: A Portrait of Paul Keating PM">Recollections of a Bleeding Heart: A Portrait of Paul Keating PM</a></i>. The book first drew criticism from Keating's by then-estranged wife, <a href="/wiki/Annita_Keating" class="mw-redirect" title="Annita Keating">Annita Keating</a>, who said that it understated her contribution, a complaint Watson rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Keating himself was so unhappy with the book that it brought the two men's friendship to an abrupt end.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating initially avoided public political comment during the Howard government, although made occasional speeches criticising his successor's social policies. Ahead of the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a>, Keating joined former Labor Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> to campaign against Howard, describing Howard as a "desiccated coconut" who was "<a href="/wiki/Araldite" title="Araldite">Araldited</a> to the seat", as an "...old <a href="/wiki/Antediluvian" title="Antediluvian">antediluvian</a> 19th century person who wanted to stomp forever...on ordinary people's rights to organise themselves at work...he's a pre-<a href="/wiki/Copernican_Revolution" title="Copernican Revolution">Copernican</a> <a href="/wiki/Obscurantism" title="Obscurantism">obscurantist</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-Middle-of-the-road_fascists_can&#39;t_compose_IR_policy_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middle-of-the-road_fascists_can&#39;t_compose_IR_policy-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> He also described Howard's deputy, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Costello" title="Peter Costello">Peter Costello</a>, as being "all tip and no iceberg" when referring to an alleged pact made by Howard to hand the leadership over to Costello after two terms.<sup id="cite_ref-coconut_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coconut-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2008, after Labor's victory in the 2007 election, Keating joined former prime ministers Whitlam, Fraser and Hawke in <a href="/wiki/Parliament_House,_Canberra" title="Parliament House, Canberra">Parliament House</a> to witness new Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> deliver the <a href="/wiki/Apology_to_Australia%27s_Indigenous_peoples" title="Apology to Australia&#39;s Indigenous peoples">National Apology</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Stolen_Generations" title="Stolen Generations">Stolen Generations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> In August 2008, he spoke at the book launch of <i>Unfinished Business: Paul Keating's Interrupted Revolution</i>, authored by economist David Love. Among the topics discussed during the launch were the need to increase compulsory superannuation contributions, as well as to restore incentives for people to receive their superannuation payments in annuities.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg/170px-Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg/255px-Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg/340px-Paul_Keating_2017_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="992" /></a><figcaption>Keating in 2017</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2013, Keating took part in a series of four-hour-long interviews with <a href="/wiki/Kerry_O%27Brien_(journalist)" title="Kerry O&#39;Brien (journalist)">Kerry O'Brien</a> which were broadcast on the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">ABC</a> in November of that year. The series covered Keating's early life, his entry into Parliament, his years as treasurer and prime minister, and canvassing his academic, musical and artistic interests, economic and cultural vision for Australia, and commitment to Australia's integration into Asia. O'Brien used these conversations as the basis for a 2014 book <i>Keating: The Interviews</i>. Keating repeatedly declared he would not write a memoir, so his cooperation with O'Brien was perceived as the closest he would come to producing an autobiography. In 2016, Troy Bramston, a journalist for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i> and a political historian, wrote an unauthorised biography that Keating cooperated with titled <i>Paul Keating: The Big-Picture Leader</i>. Bramston was given full access to Keating's personal papers, was granted a series of interviews with Keating and also interviewed more than 100 other people. It was described as the "authoritative" and "definitive" Keating biography written by a "first class" political historian.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/2015_New_South_Wales_state_election" title="2015 New South Wales state election">2015 New South Wales state election</a>, Keating gave his support for the privatisation agenda of the Liberal government and slammed the Labor Party for its anti-privatisation position.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2019, during campaigning for that year's <a href="/wiki/2019_Australian_federal_election" title="2019 Australian federal election">federal election</a>, Keating spoke out against the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Security_Intelligence_Organisation" title="Australian Security Intelligence Organisation">Australian Security Intelligence Organisation</a> by calling them "nutters".<sup id="cite_ref-nutter2019_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nutter2019-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> His remarks attracted media criticism, and Labor Leader <a href="/wiki/Bill_Shorten" title="Bill Shorten">Bill Shorten</a> distanced himself from Keating's views.<sup id="cite_ref-3AW2019_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3AW2019-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Keating later issued a joint statement with <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> endorsing Labor's economic plan as part of the election campaign, and condemning the Liberal Party for "completely [giving] up the economic reform agenda". They stated that "Shorten's Labor is the only party of government focused on the need to modernise the economy to deal with the major challenge of our time: human induced climate change"; it was the first joint press statement released by the two since 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> After Hawke's death in the same month, Keating gave an address at Hawke's <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state memorial</a> service at <a href="/wiki/Sydney_Opera_House" title="Sydney Opera House">Sydney Opera House</a> on 14 June, where he reflected on the "great friendship and partnership" the two had enjoyed.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2021, following the announcement of the <a href="/wiki/AUKUS" title="AUKUS">AUKUS</a> trilateral military alliance between the United States, United Kingdom and Australia, Keating criticised the alliance, saying that "Australia turns its back on the 21st century, the century of Asia, for the jaded and faded Anglosphere" and the deal would be "locking the country and its military forces into the force structure of the United States by acquiring US submarines". Keating went on to criticize Labor's opposition foreign affairs spokesperson <a href="/wiki/Penny_Wong" title="Penny Wong">Penny Wong</a>, accusing the Labor opposition of being complicit with the Liberal government in "false representation of China’s foreign policy".<sup id="cite_ref-guardian-20211110_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian-20211110-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> His comments were criticised by Labor MPs <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Byrne_(politician)" title="Anthony Byrne (politician)">Anthony Byrne</a> and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Khalil" title="Peter Khalil">Peter Khalil</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-smh-20210923_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smh-20210923-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> In January 2022, Keating accused British Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/Liz_Truss" title="Liz Truss">Liz Truss</a> of making "demented" comments about Chinese military aggression in the Pacific, saying that "Britain suffers delusions of grandeur and relevance deprivation."<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In 2023, Keating went on to call the AUKUS pact "the worst deal in all history" and lambasting the Labor government for being "incompetent" and stating that the decision was the worst by a Labor government since <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a> attempted to introduce conscription during World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1976, Keating married <a href="/wiki/Annita_van_Iersel" title="Annita van Iersel">Annita van Iersel</a>, a Dutch-born flight attendant for <a href="/wiki/Alitalia" title="Alitalia">Alitalia</a>. They had four children, who spent some of their teenage years in <a href="/wiki/The_Lodge_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Lodge (Australia)">The Lodge</a>, the prime minister's official residence in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>. The couple separated in November 1998. While they did not formally divorce until 2008, Annita had resumed her maiden name long before then. Before his marriage to van Iersel, Keating had in 1972 announced his engagement to fashion consultant Kristine Kennedy, but they did not marry.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Since 1998, Keating's partner has been the actress <a href="/wiki/Julieanne_Newbould" title="Julieanne Newbould">Julieanne Newbould</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Keating's daughter, Katherine Keating, is a former adviser to former New South Wales Minister <a href="/wiki/Craig_Knowles" title="Craig Knowles">Craig Knowles</a> as well as former <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Premier" class="mw-redirect" title="New South Wales Premier">New South Wales Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>.
</p><p>In the early 1970s, Keating moved from the family home in Bankstown when he purchased a new brick-veneer house at 12 Gerard Avenue, <a href="/wiki/Condell_Park" title="Condell Park">Condell Park</a>, two doors up from his parents' new home at No. 8 Gerard Avenue.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> This became the family home after his marriage in 1976 until 1983, when the Keatings sold the property for $123,000 and moved to a one-storey rental house in the <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a> suburb of <a href="/wiki/Red_Hill,_Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Red Hill, Australian Capital Territory">Red Hill</a> to be closer to work.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Keating's interests include the music of <a href="/wiki/Gustav_Mahler" title="Gustav Mahler">Gustav Mahler</a> and collecting French <a href="/wiki/Antique" title="Antique">antique</a> clocks.<sup id="cite_ref-bio_15-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bio-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> He currently resides in <a href="/wiki/Potts_Point" class="mw-redirect" title="Potts Point">Potts Point</a>, in inner-city Sydney, and has a holiday home on the <a href="/wiki/Hawkesbury_River" title="Hawkesbury River">Hawkesbury River</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture">Popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Paul_Keating&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 2005, <i><a href="/wiki/Keating!" title="Keating!">Keating!</a></i>, a musical based on Keating's life and career, premiered at the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_International_Comedy_Festival" title="Melbourne International Comedy Festival">Melbourne International Comedy Festival</a>. It went on to run until 2010, winning a number of awards and being broadcast on <a href="/wiki/ABC2" class="mw-redirect" title="ABC2">ABC2</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-arse_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-arse-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
